What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I’m here because I feel like I’m always running hot, if that makes sense. Like there’s a motor in me that just doesn’t shut off. I can be sitting there, or trying to relax, or even just going through a normal day at the firehouse, and inside it feels like I’m already revved up, like I’m waiting for the next thing before the last thing is even done.\n\nAnd lately it’s started causing problems at work. At the firehouse, you know, there’s already a lot of intensity, and I’m used to that, but this feels different. I feel like I’m constantly on, constantly moving, and it’s been getting me in trouble there. So I figured I needed to actually talk to somebody instead of just trying to muscle through it like I usually do.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious or worried when you're in this revved up state?\n\nPatient: I don’t know that I’d call it worry, exactly. Like, I’m not usually sitting there thinking, “Oh no, something bad is going to happen,” or running through some specific fear in my head. It’s more… my body feels amped up first, if that makes sense, and then my brain kind of tries to find somewhere to put that energy.\n\nSometimes I’ll be tense or irritated because I can’t settle, but I don’t know if that’s anxiety or just being wound up. I guess I’m not always good at separating those things. But it doesn’t feel like classic worrying to me, more like being stuck with the engine racing even when I don’t need to be going anywhere.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself repeating certain actions or rituals over and over?\n\nPatient: Um… not that I can think of, no. I mean, I have routines like anybody does, especially with work, because at the firehouse you kind of have to do things a certain way and check equipment and all that, but I don’t think I’m doing rituals over and over because I feel like I have to or something bad will happen.\n\nI might double back sometimes because I forgot what I was doing or I’ll recheck something if I’m not sure I actually did it, but I wouldn’t call it a ritual. It’s more like, “Did I just do that or did I only think about doing it?” But no, I haven’t noticed anything like repeating actions in a specific way over and over.\n\nDoctor: Do you find it hard to focus on one thing for a long time?\n\nPatient: Yeah, definitely. That’s actually one of the things that’s been bothering me because it’s not like I’m not trying to pay attention. Like in briefings at work, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, especially if command is going over something important, and I’ll be looking right at the map or whoever’s talking. Then all of a sudden I realize I’m still staring at the map, but I didn’t actually catch half of what was just said.\n\nSame thing with drone training. I’ll be looking at the screen and trying to follow the information, and then it’s like my attention just… slips off without me noticing. I’m physically there, eyes forward, but my brain isn’t holding onto it. Then I’m playing catch-up, trying to piece together what I missed from context, which is frustrating because I don’t want to be that guy who has to ask someone to repeat something important.\n\nDoctor: Do you often find yourself fidgeting or feeling restless when you're sitting still?\n\nPatient: Yeah, all the time. Sitting still is honestly one of those things I don’t even realize I’m bad at until someone points it out or I catch myself doing it.\n\nLike if I’m in a meeting, my leg will be bouncing under the table nonstop, or I’ll be clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair, leaning forward, leaning back, messing with the edge of a paper or whatever’s sitting in front of me. Half the time I’m not doing it on purpose. I’ll suddenly notice the pen clicking and think, “Oh, that’s me,” and then I stop for maybe thirty seconds before I’m doing something else.\n\nIt’s not always that I feel panicked or anything. It’s more like my body just doesn’t want to be parked in one position. If I have to sit there and just listen, especially in a longer meeting, I feel like I need some little movement going, even if it’s just my foot bouncing or my hands fiddling with something. It’s pretty constant.\n\nDoctor: Do you often act on impulse without thinking through the consequences?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I do, and it’s usually with talking more than, like, doing some wild physical thing. At work especially, when the crew is talking through a plan or somebody’s explaining how we’re going to approach something, sometimes the answer or what I think is the answer is already in my head, and I just jump in before I’ve really thought about whether it’s my turn to talk.\n\nIt’s not like I’m trying to be disrespectful. In the moment it feels like, “Oh, I know where this is going,” or “We should mention this before we forget,” and then my mouth is already moving. Then a second later I realize I cut across someone, or I stepped on what they were saying, or I kind of messed up the rhythm of the discussion. And then I feel bad because in the firehouse, communication matters, and people need to be able to finish a thought without me barging in.\n\nI’ll catch myself after the fact and think, “Man, why didn’t you just wait two more seconds?” But in the moment, it’s like there isn’t much of a pause between having the thought and saying it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and restlessness are equally bothersome to you?\n\nPatient: I think they kind of feed into each other, so it’s hard to separate them cleanly. But if I’m being honest, the focus part is the one that’s really starting to hurt me at work.\n\nThe restlessness is annoying, and I know it can make me look distracted, but the focus issue is what’s costing me trust at the firehouse. It’s gotten to the point where I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting, which was… honestly pretty embarrassing. Not just embarrassing, but scary, because this isn’t a job where missing details is just inconvenient. People need to know I’m tracking what’s being said, that I caught the plan, that I remember the important parts.\n\nAnd now it feels like my shot at moving into leadership is slipping away. I’ve been trying to work toward that, and I care about it, but if the crew feels like they can’t count on me to consistently keep up with details, then why would they put me in that position? That’s the part that bothers me the most. It’s not just me feeling restless in a chair — it’s starting to affect how people see me and whether they trust me.\n\nDoctor: Are your difficulties with focus and restlessness affecting your relationships with friends or family outside of work?\n\nPatient: Yeah, it does. Not in a dramatic way like we’re constantly fighting about it, but it definitely shows up at home.\n\nEven on my days off, when I’m supposed to be relaxing or just spending time with my family, I have a really hard time doing quiet, low-key stuff. Like if we’re sitting around watching something together, or playing a game, or just hanging out, I’ll start off with good intentions, but then before long I’m talking over the show, getting up to check something, wandering into the garage, messing with tools, organizing something that didn’t need organizing right then.\n\nIt’s like I’ll sit down, and within a few minutes part of me is already looking for the next thing to do. I’ll think, “Oh, I should check that tool,” or “I need to move that thing in the garage,” and then I’m up. And sometimes my family is like, “Can you just sit with us?” And I want to. I really do. I’m not trying to avoid them. But sitting still and just being quiet with them feels way harder than it should.\n\nI also catch myself talking when maybe everyone else is trying to have a calmer moment. Like I’ll fill the space with comments or questions or random thoughts, and then I realize I’m kind of disrupting the whole thing. So yeah, it affects family time, mostly because I have trouble just being present and settled with them.\n\nDoctor: Did you experience these feelings of being revved up or having trouble sitting still when you were a kid, before you turned 12?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being that young, obviously, but my mom has told me a bunch of times that I was like this even back in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always the kid popping up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or blurting out answers before the teacher finished asking the question. And it wasn’t because I couldn’t understand the work — she always makes that point, like, “You were bright enough to do it.” It was more that I’d lose the homework, or forget to turn it in, or I’d get notes sent home because I couldn’t stay settled or I was talking out of turn.\n\nI remember some of that too, mostly the feeling of being in trouble for stuff that didn’t feel intentional. Like I wasn’t trying to be a problem kid. I just had this urge to answer, or move, or do something, and then afterward it was like, “Why did I do that again?” So yeah, from what I remember and what my mom says, it was definitely there before I was 12.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced these feelings of being revved up or having trouble sitting still consistently throughout your life, or have there been periods where they were less noticeable?\n\nPatient: It’s been there in some form for as long as I can remember, but if you’re asking when it became a real, hard-to-manage problem, I’d say about a year and five months ago.\n\nThat’s when things changed with the longer rotating shifts, and then the extra documentation got added, and the drone duties on top of that. Before that, I could kind of work around it better, or maybe it just didn’t stand out as much because the routine was more familiar. But once the schedule got longer and less predictable, and there were more tasks where I had to sit down, track details, document things properly, switch gears, remember what was assigned — that’s when it really started becoming obvious and causing problems.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say there were times where it completely went away. It was always kind of part of me. But it’s been really noticeable and disruptive for about the last year and five months, since those job changes piled on.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting are more troublesome to you than your difficulties with focus and paying attention?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part probably causes bigger problems overall, but the sitting still and fidgeting thing is really obvious and embarrassing in the moment.\n\nLike, if I’m sitting in a meeting, it’s almost automatic. My leg is bouncing under the table, or I’m clicking a pen, shifting around in the chair, tapping something, or messing with whatever’s in front of me. Sometimes I don’t even notice I’m doing it until somebody looks over, or I suddenly realize the pen has been clicking for who knows how long.\n\nSo I don’t know if I’d say it’s more troublesome than paying attention, exactly, but it’s more visible. The focus problems mess me up more in terms of getting things done, but the fidgeting makes me feel like I can’t just appear calm and normal in a room. It’s like my body is giving me away even when I’m trying to sit there and be professional.\n\nDoctor: Did your mom or any other family members notice these behaviors, like being revved up or having trouble sitting still, when you were younger than 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, my mom definitely noticed it when I was really young. I don’t know if she could pinpoint, like, “before age 7” exactly, but she talks about grade school like it was already very much there.\n\nShe’s told me I was always the kid getting up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, blurting out answers before the teacher was done, that kind of thing. And she always says it wasn’t that I couldn’t do the work — she’ll say, “You were bright enough, you just couldn’t keep yourself organized or still.” I’d lose homework, forget to turn things in, and then notes would come home from the teacher about me talking out of turn or not staying settled.\n\nSo, yeah, from what my mom has said, it wasn’t something that just started later. It sounds like it was already showing up back in early grade school.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any of these behaviors, such as being revved up or having trouble sitting still, before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, from what my mom says, I did. I mean, I don’t have a clear memory of being, like, six years old and thinking, “I can’t sit still,” but she’s told me plenty of stories from when I was in grade school.\n\nShe says I was always the kid popping up out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to, or blurting out answers before the teacher finished asking the question. And it wasn’t that I couldn’t understand the work — she’s said that a lot, actually, that I was bright enough to do it — but I’d lose homework, forget to hand it in, or just be all over the place with keeping track of things. There were notes sent home about me talking out of turn or not staying settled.\n\nSo, yeah, based on what she’s told me, this was definitely showing up when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Did you exhibit any symptoms like being revved up, having trouble sitting still, or difficulty with focus before you were 12 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I know I already mentioned some of this, but from what my mom says, it was definitely there back in grade school, not something that just showed up when I got older.\n\nShe’s always described me as the kid who couldn’t stay in his seat — like I’d be up when I wasn’t supposed to be, or I’d blurt out answers before the teacher even finished asking the question. And then the focus side was there too, because I’d lose homework or forget to turn it in, and notes would come home about that kind of stuff. The frustrating part is she says I was bright enough to do the work, so it wasn’t like I didn’t understand it. It was more that I couldn’t keep myself settled or keep track of everything the way I was supposed to.\n\nSo, yeah, before 12, definitely. My mom makes it sound like it was already pretty obvious when I was pretty young.\n\nDoctor: Did your symptoms of being revved up or having trouble sitting still start before you were 7 years old?\n\nPatient: Yeah, from what my mom says, yes. I know I’ve kind of said this already, but she’s been pretty consistent about it — she says I was like that even back in grade school.\n\nShe talks about me being the kid who was always out of my seat when I wasn’t supposed to be, or blurting out answers before the teacher was done. And I guess the homework thing was a big one too — losing it, forgetting it, not turning it in — and then notes getting sent home from school about it. She always makes a point of saying I was bright enough to do the work, which honestly makes it a little more frustrating to hear, because it wasn’t like I didn’t understand what was going on. I just couldn’t seem to keep myself settled or keep track of things the way I was supposed to.\n\nSo, yeah, I can’t personally remember every detail from before 7, but based on what my mom has told me, it definitely sounds like it started that early.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever been diagnosed with attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) or do you think you might have it?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve ever had, like, a formal diagnosis where somebody sat me down and said, “This is definitely what you have,” at least not that I can remember.\n\nAs far as whether I think I might have it… I mean, that’s kind of why I’m here, I guess. I’ve wondered about it, but I also don’t want to just diagnose myself off things I’ve read or stuff people say. Some of it sounds familiar to me, and some of the stuff from when I was a kid makes me think maybe there’s something there, but I don’t know. I’d rather have you sort through it properly than me just slap a label on myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your symptoms are more related to inattention, hyperactivity, or a mix of both?\n\nPatient: I’d say it feels like a mix, but the thing that’s pushing me to actually be here is more that “running hot” feeling. Like I’m always revved up inside, like there’s a motor going and I can’t quite shut it off.\n\nAnd lately that’s been becoming more of a problem at the firehouse. I can usually tell myself, “Okay, calm down, slow down,” but it doesn’t always work. It’s like my body and brain are already moving before I’ve fully gotten a handle on myself. So I don’t know if I’d separate it cleanly into one or the other, but the constant activity piece is what feels most obvious to me right now, and it’s starting to get me in trouble.\n\nDoctor: Have you often interrupted others when they were speaking, like finishing their sentences for them?\n\nPatient: Yeah. I wish I could say no, but yeah, I do that more than I should.\n\nIt happens a lot at the firehouse when we’re talking through a plan or somebody’s explaining how they want something handled. If I think I already know where they’re going, or the answer pops into my head, I just jump in. It’s not always me trying to be rude or take over, at least not intentionally. It’s more like the thought is already there and my mouth starts before I’ve really checked whether it’s my turn.\n\nAnd then, a second later, I can see it. Like somebody pauses, or the conversation gets thrown off, or I realize I basically stepped on what they were trying to say. Sometimes I’m finishing the point they were making, sometimes I’m cutting in with what I think the solution is, and then afterward I’m like, “Why didn’t I just wait ten seconds?”\n\nIt’s embarrassing, honestly, because in that setting the flow of communication matters. It’s not just casual talking. If guys are working through a plan and I cut across it, even if what I’m saying is useful, it can mess up the rhythm of the discussion. And I can tell it annoys people. So, yeah, interrupting is definitely a thing for me.\n\nDoctor: Have your symptoms significantly impacted your ability to function at the firehouse or in your personal relationships?\n\nPatient: Yeah… at this point, yes. I don’t think I can honestly minimize that anymore.\n\nAt the firehouse especially, it’s gone past just being, you know, “he’s a little scattered” or “he’s got a lot of energy.” It’s started costing me trust. That’s probably the part that bothers me the most, because in that job trust is everything. The crew has to know you’re tracking what’s going on, that you caught the details, that if something was said in a briefing or a plan, you’re going to hold onto it and act on it.\n\nAnd I’ve had enough instances where I missed pieces or didn’t keep things straight consistently that it became an actual issue. I got pulled into a performance and safety meeting over it, which was… pretty humiliating, honestly. Not because they were wrong to do it, but because sitting there and hearing it laid out like that made it feel real in a way I couldn’t dodge.\n\nAnd now I feel like my shot at moving into more of a leadership role is slipping away. I’d been hoping that was the direction I was heading, but if the crew doesn’t feel like they can count on me to reliably track the details, then why would they put me in charge of anybody else? That’s the part that hits hard. It’s not just annoying people or having a bad habit. It’s affecting how people see me professionally, and it’s affecting what I might be allowed to do next.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your difficulties with focus and paying attention are more prominent than your difficulties with sitting still and fidgeting?\n\nPatient: I think the focus part is probably more prominent in the sense that it causes the bigger problems, even though the “can’t sit still” feeling is more obvious to me day to day.\n\nLike, I can feel when I’m fidgety or restless. That’s right there. But the attention thing sneaks up on me, and then I realize after the fact that I missed something important. In briefings, or especially with drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, really trying to follow everything. I’ll be looking at the map or the screen like I’m paying attention, and then all of a sudden I realize command has been talking and I didn’t catch half of what was just said.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m bored or don’t care. That’s what frustrates me. I’m trying to listen, and my eyes are pointed at the right thing, but somewhere in there I kind of drift off or get stuck staring, and then I have to piece together what I missed. So yeah, the restlessness is there, but the focus and paying attention part is probably the one that’s hurting me more.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. In briefings, or especially with drone training, I’ll be locked in for the first few minutes, really trying to follow everything. I’ll be looking at the map or the screen like I’m paying attention, and then all of a sudden I realize command has been talking and I didn’t catch half of what was just said.\n\nAnd it’s not like I’m bored or don’t care. That’s what frustrates me. I’m trying to listen, and my eyes are pointed at the right thing, but somewhere in there I kind of drift off or get stuck staring, and then I have to piece together what I missed. So yeah, the restlessness is there, but the focus and paying attention part is probably the one that’s hurting me more."
  }
]
